# Literature review

This document collects the literature, software and standards evidence on which the evoparam specification rests. It covers fourteen axes, from spectral parameterization and ERP measurement through stimulation artifacts, CCEP methods, other evoked-response domains, open datasets, fitting methods and time-frequency analysis to naming, licensing, citation practice, user needs and ecosystem standards. It lists 1,087 numbered references. Each axis has a synthesis followed by its numbered reference list. The gap analysis that draws on this review is in `GAPS.md`.

**Access dates.** Sources were consulted on 2026-10-06. A verification pass on 2026-10-07 re-checked DOIs, authors, titles and selected citation counts against registries (Crossref, OpenAlex, PubMed) and publisher pages. Unless stated otherwise in an entry, "accessed" means 2026-10-06.

**Reference keys.** Every reference has a key of the form `[LIT-<axis>-<n>]`, for example `[LIT-ccep-11]`. Here `<axis>` is one of `fooof`, `erptools`, `ccep`, `artifact`, `fitting`, `datasets`, `domains`, `tf`, `variability`, `naming`, `erpmeas`, `citation`, `needs` and `standards`, and `<n>` is the reference number in that axis's research file (`research/<file>.md`, named under each section). The numbering is kept exactly as in the research files, so a key resolves to the same item there. Some works appear under more than one axis; these entries carry a note that points to the other key. The specification and `GAPS.md` cite these keys.

**Verification legend.** Each entry ends with a status tag.
- **VERIFIED**: the bibliographic record and the claims attributed to it were checked against the source, a registry record or the repository/page itself on the access date.
- **VERIFIED (in part)**: part of the record was confirmed (for example a preprint or abstract), but another part (for example the journal DOI) was not.
- **VERIFIED (corrected 2026-10-07)**: the 2026-10-07 verification pass found an error in the DOI, author list, title, venue or year. The entry shows the corrected record, and a note states what was changed.
- **VERIFIED (re-checked 2026-10-07)**: an entry first recorded as unverified was confirmed by the registry check.
- **VERIFIED (count updated 2026-10-07)** / **VERIFIED (count retrieved 2026-10-07)**: the record was already verified, and its citation count was replaced by, or newly given as, the 2026-10-07 OpenAlex value.
- **UNVERIFIED**: the record or the specific numbers could not be confirmed. Such entries must be checked before any number from them is quoted. Where the registry check also failed, a note says so.
- **UNVERIFIED; placeholder, not citable**: the identity of the item was not resolved. The entry is kept only so that the numbering stays intact.

The corrections of `research/verification.md` (2026-10-07) are applied throughout. That pass covered eleven of the fourteen research files. The files for the citation, needs and standards axes were not re-checked, so their tags are those recorded on 2026-10-06.

**Citation counts.** Citation counts are always given with their source and retrieval date. For the FOOOF/specparam paper (Donoghue et al. 2020, Nature Neuroscience) this document uses a single value everywhere: 2,533 citations (OpenAlex, 2026-10-07), with a per-year series for 2021–2026 of 178/283/425/506/600/472, where 2026 is a partial year. Counts for other works are as recorded on 2026-10-06 unless an entry says otherwise.

**Contents.**
1. Spectral parameterization (FOOOF/specparam) and ERPparam (`fooof`, 106 references)
2. ERP and evoked-response software (`erptools`, 88 references)
3. Cortico-cortical evoked potentials: detection and measurement (`ccep`, 71 references)
4. Stimulation artifact removal (`artifact`, 71 references)
5. Curve fitting, model selection and uncertainty (`fitting`, 55 references)
6. Open datasets for evoked responses (`datasets`, 75 references)
7. Other evoked-response domains (TMS-EEG, DBS, IONM, peripheral, BCI) (`domains`, 86 references)
8. Time-frequency analysis of evoked responses (`tf`, 68 references)
9. Trial-by-trial variability, brain state and animal evoked responses (`variability`, 82 references)
10. Package naming, licensing and intellectual property (`naming`, 60 references)
11. ERP measurement methodology and single-trial estimation (`erpmeas`, 70 references)
12. What makes a neuroscience tool highly cited (`citation`, 57 references)
13. Unmet user needs in issue trackers, forums and reviews (`needs`, 98 references)
14. Standards and ecosystem integration (`standards`, 100 references)


## 1. Spectral parameterization (FOOOF/specparam) and ERPparam

Source file: `research/fooof-erpparam.md`. Keys: `[LIT-fooof-1]` to `[LIT-fooof-106]`.

### Synthesis

This axis treats FOOOF (renamed specparam) as the adoption model for an evoked-response tool, and ERPparam as the only public attempt to move that model from power spectra to time-domain waveforms.

**Citation record.** The reference paper [LIT-fooof-1] was preceded by a 2018 preprint [LIT-fooof-2] and by a 1.0.0 release in April 2020, seven months before the journal version [LIT-fooof-8]. OpenAlex counts 2,533 citations on 2026-10-07: 178, 283, 425, 506, 600 and 472 in 2021 to 2026, the last being a partial year [LIT-fooof-3]. The curve was still rising five years after publication. A 2025 systematic review of aperiodic activity in clinical studies found the method in 67% of reports. Yet only 56% of those reports documented the settings, and 30% reported a goodness-of-fit check [LIT-fooof-4], even though the package includes a methods-text generator [LIT-fooof-17]. Built-in reporting therefore helps but is not enough. It has to be the default output, not an optional call.

**What made it adoptable.**
- One object with `fit` and `report` calls and a small, stable set of settings [LIT-fooof-14][LIT-fooof-15].
- numpy and scipy as the only required dependencies [LIT-fooof-14].
- Sequential tutorials, one of them about reporting and referencing [LIT-fooof-21].
- Validation on simulated spectra with 1,000 spectra per condition, deliberate model violations and a comparison with nine human raters [LIT-fooof-1].
- A simulation module that later methods papers reused [LIT-fooof-19][LIT-fooof-31][LIT-fooof-33].

The old and new package names together reach about 80,000 downloads a month [LIT-fooof-12][LIT-fooof-13].

**Weaknesses that a successor should not repeat.**
- Knee fitting produces negative or unstable knees and convergence failures, and an alternative functional form has been proposed [LIT-fooof-40][LIT-fooof-41][LIT-fooof-42][LIT-fooof-43][LIT-fooof-44].
- Users stumble over log versus linear units and over the two-stage peak estimates [LIT-fooof-45][LIT-fooof-46][LIT-fooof-47]. Tickets on defaults and on a benchmarking system are still open [LIT-fooof-48][LIT-fooof-49].
- Published critiques show that high-frequency plateaus, peaks at the range border and overlapping peaks bias the estimates [LIT-fooof-50]. Test-retest reliability differs by parameter [LIT-fooof-52], and allowing more peaks lowers the reliability of the aperiodic estimates [LIT-fooof-53].
- Biophysical modelling warns against detrending without a physiological justification [LIT-fooof-54]. Comparisons of aperiodic measures show that the results depend on the frequency range and model form [LIT-fooof-55].
- The fits return point estimates only. Bayesian spectral models [LIT-fooof-37] and model selection by information criteria [LIT-fooof-33] show that the field wants uncertainty and evidence for each peak.
- Version 2.0 has been in release-candidate status for 30 months [LIT-fooof-9][LIT-fooof-10], with delays attributed to a single maintainer [LIT-fooof-11]. The MATLAB ports and wrappers have drifted from the reference code [LIT-fooof-16][LIT-fooof-27][LIT-fooof-29][LIT-fooof-30].

**ERPparam.** The repository is a fork created on 2023-07-06 [LIT-fooof-58]. It has 4 stars, no PyPI release and no documentation site, and it still carries the parent's version string and spectral tutorials [LIT-fooof-39][LIT-fooof-59][LIT-fooof-60][LIT-fooof-81][LIT-fooof-85]. Its only public presentation is a 2023 conference abstract [LIT-fooof-69]. Reading the source shows a sum of Gaussian or skewed-Gaussian peaks in time with no explicit slow background term, and a baseline taken from the pre-stimulus window [LIT-fooof-74][LIT-fooof-76].

The CCEP review's tool table describes it instead as an aperiodic-plus-Gaussian model [LIT-ccep-68]. This review follows the source-level reading. The open issues show the weak points:
- heuristic peak corrections that drop real peaks [LIT-fooof-88][LIT-fooof-89]
- slow skewed fits [LIT-fooof-90]
- a request for traditional ERP measures so that results can be compared [LIT-fooof-91]

**Neighbouring methods.**
- Canonical response parameterization gives a shared shape and per-trial weights for stimulation responses, with significance tests, but no component timing [LIT-fooof-92].
- Latency-variability decomposition, overlap deconvolution and per-sample regression do not model component shapes [LIT-fooof-96][LIT-fooof-97][LIT-fooof-98].
- Bayesian trial-level waveform models exist only as statistical papers, not as packaged workflows [LIT-fooof-99][LIT-fooof-100].

**Implications.** A successor should:
- ship a released, citable package from the start
- model components over an explicit slow background
- report uncertainty and a response-existence test
- make the methods text a default output
- publish a benchmark
- provide classical measures alongside the model parameters
- use SI units and documented presets
- read and write MNE, BIDS and NWB natively
- keep a name that will not change

## 2. ERP and evoked-response software

Source file: `research/erp-toolboxes.md`. Keys: `[LIT-erptools-1]` to `[LIT-erptools-88]`.

### Synthesis

This axis surveys the general EEG/MEG environments and the ERP add-ons that researchers use to turn evoked waveforms into numbers. It checks each one for model fitting, uncertainty, single-trial scoring, stimulation-artifact handling and licence.

**The measurement vocabulary is ERPLAB's.** The ERPLAB Measurement Tool defines the measures most ERP papers report [LIT-erptools-1][LIT-erptools-2][LIT-erptools-3]:
- instantaneous, peak (with an optional local-peak rule), mean and four area amplitudes
- fractional-area and fractional-peak latencies

ERPLAB is GPL-3.0 MATLAB code [LIT-erptools-6]. Its 2014 paper has about 2,750 citations in OpenAlex (2026-10-06), at roughly 300 per year [LIT-erptools-9]. Its reach comes from being an EEGLAB plug-in: EEGLAB users asking about peak latency are sent to ERPLAB [LIT-erptools-12][LIT-erptools-13].

None of the six measurement families is a fitted model. They are geometric features of an average, returned without uncertainty and only for averaged bins [LIT-erptools-2][LIT-erptools-3].

**Python.**
- MNE-Python is the dominant stack (BSD-3, about 3,500 stars) [LIT-erptools-18]. Its long-standing primitive is `Evoked.get_peak`, which returns the extremum in a window [LIT-erptools-17]. The ERP tutorial computes mean amplitude by hand [LIT-erptools-19].
- The 1.13 API reference now lists a small `mne.stats.erp` namespace with peak, area, fractional-latency and standardized measurement error functions [LIT-needs-4][LIT-fooof-94]. This is a minimal answer to a request that stayed open for six years [LIT-needs-2].
- Stimulation-artifact handling in MNE is limited to window interpolation (`fix_stim_artifact`) [LIT-erptools-20].
- Statistics are sample-wise cluster permutation tests [LIT-erptools-21][LIT-erptools-85].

**MATLAB environments.**
- FieldTrip and Brainstorm have no ERP peak tools. Users are pointed to `findpeaks` or custom code [LIT-erptools-25][LIT-erptools-31][LIT-erptools-33]. FieldTrip's TMS tutorial uses NaN replacement, interpolation and ICA, and states that it has no TEP peak tool [LIT-erptools-27].
- Letswave advertises single-trial tools whose documentation is a stub [LIT-erptools-40][LIT-erptools-41].
- The ERP PCA Toolkit offers temporal PCA under GPL [LIT-erptools-45][LIT-erptools-48].
- SPM's only fitted model of evoked responses is dynamic causal modelling, which is a network model, not a scoring method [LIT-erptools-76][LIT-erptools-77].
- Liesefeld's `latency.m` adds robust area latencies and jackknife scoring (GPL) [LIT-erptools-81][LIT-erptools-82]. The jackknife procedure for latency differences is documented in [LIT-erptools-84].

**Regression and reliability tools.**
- Unfold deconvolves overlapping responses with spline regression. The MATLAB version is GPL [LIT-erptools-53][LIT-erptools-56] and the Julia version is MIT [LIT-erptools-54].
- UnfoldSim.jl is the only simulation framework in this tool family [LIT-erptools-57].
- The temporal-response-function toolboxes model continuous stimuli [LIT-erptools-59][LIT-erptools-61], with a Python port [LIT-erptools-63].
- LIMO fits hierarchical GLMs to single trials but reports regression weights, not component parameters [LIT-erptools-64][LIT-erptools-65][LIT-erptools-67].
- Reliability analysis needs the retired ERA Toolbox or its beta successor, both GPL and dependent on Stan [LIT-erptools-69][LIT-erptools-70][LIT-erptools-71]. This matters because psychometric critiques ask for reliability reporting [LIT-erptools-72].

**Licence partition.** The mature scoring code is copyleft MATLAB: ERPLAB, the ERP PCA Toolkit and `latency.m`. The permissively licensed environments (MNE, LIMO, Unfold.jl, eegUtils) contain little or no scoring [LIT-erptools-51][LIT-erptools-64]. Apache-2.0 code cannot absorb GPLv3 code [LIT-erptools-87], so a permissive package must re-implement the published definitions rather than port them.

**Implications.** The gaps that every toolbox shares are:
- a parametric component model
- uncertainty on scored values
- single-trial parameters with pooling
- a component-level existence test
- model-aware artifact handling
- a tidy output schema
- a benchmark of scoring methods on common synthetic data

A Python implementation of the ERPLAB vocabulary, computed on MNE objects and validated against ERPLAB on shared data, is the cheapest feature with the largest audience. Placing those measures beside the model parameters links the new method to how researchers already report results. For scale, FOOOF/specparam reached 2,533 citations (OpenAlex, 2026-10-07) [LIT-erptools-88]. The ERP toolboxes are cited as environments, not as named methods.

## 3. Cortico-cortical evoked potentials: detection and measurement

Source file: `research/ccep-methods.md`. Keys: `[LIT-ccep-1]` to `[LIT-ccep-71]`.

### Synthesis

This axis reviews how cortico-cortical evoked potentials (CCEPs) from single-pulse electrical stimulation (SPES) are defined, detected and measured, and why results do not transfer between centres.

**No operational definition of a response.** The original description measured N1 and N2 by visual peak-to-trough rules [LIT-ccep-1]. A later review gives the usual windows: N1 at 10–50 ms and N2 at 50–300 ms. The same review states that there is no consensus on how to measure them [LIT-ccep-2]. In practice the definitions vary widely [LIT-ccep-3][LIT-ccep-5][LIT-ccep-6][LIT-ccep-7][LIT-ccep-9][LIT-ccep-10][LIT-ccep-12][LIT-ccep-13][LIT-ccep-14]:
- windows start between 5 and 16 ms
- N1 windows end between 40 and 100 ms
- z thresholds range from 2.6 to 6 SD

The systematic review of 187 full-text studies found that 49.7% did not describe how responses were detected. Of the 72 studies that did, 68.1% used threshold rules, which "assume fixed response shapes". Only 13 of the 72 shared their data and 3 shared their code [LIT-ccep-11].

Other reviews describe the same gaps:
- Later reviews call latency windows and feature extraction open questions [LIT-ccep-24].
- A clinical survey describes substantial variability between centres [LIT-ccep-25].
- The intracranial EEG guidelines contain no CCEP detection recommendation [LIT-ccep-26].

**Human agreement sets the ceiling.**
- The ER-detect validation scored 59,178 responses. Human raters agreed with a kappa of 0.60, and automated detectors reached kappas of 0.39–0.59 [LIT-ccep-8]. ER-detect itself is a GPL Python package with defaults tuned at about 0.2 Hz stimulation [LIT-ccep-28][LIT-ccep-30].
- F-TRACT processed hundreds of patients into an atlas of latencies and durations [LIT-ccep-31][LIT-ccep-32][LIT-ccep-33]. The same consortium showed that threshold responses barely reproduce between identical trains: at Z = 3, the correlation between paired measurements approaches zero at long distances [LIT-ccep-14].

**Data-driven alternatives.**
- Canonical response parameterization (CRP) projects single trials onto a canonical shape and tests significance. It explicitly "cannot parameterize feature timing" [LIT-ccep-15][LIT-ccep-35].
- Basis profile curves group stimulation sites by shared motifs [LIT-ccep-16][LIT-ccep-17][LIT-ccep-36][LIT-ccep-37].
- A Python port of CRP sits inside a sleep toolbox [LIT-ccep-38][LIT-ccep-39].

**Spectral measures form a separate literature.** Examples are high-gamma signal-to-noise ratios [LIT-ccep-40], early fast components specific to the seizure-onset zone [LIT-ccep-41][LIT-ccep-42], PSD-based connectivity [LIT-ccep-21] and travelling waves [LIT-ccep-22].

**Referencing and stimulation parameters change the numbers.**
- Adjusted common averaging (CARLA) exists only in MATLAB [LIT-ccep-18][LIT-ccep-43][LIT-ccep-44].
- A contaminated reference alters peak-latency distributions [LIT-ccep-45], and montage choice moved the N1 latency from 47 to 71 ms in one paediatric series [LIT-ccep-27].
- Bipolar and common-average montages disagree beyond about 2 cm [LIT-ccep-46]. Volume conduction can be estimated from the artifact itself [LIT-ccep-47][LIT-ccep-48].
- Amplitude grows nonlinearly with current, charge and pulse width and saturates at 6–7 mA [LIT-ccep-5][LIT-ccep-49][LIT-ccep-50]. Local and distant responses depend on different properties [LIT-ccep-51][LIT-ccep-52].

**Variability and state.**
- Trial-to-trial SD is higher in seizure-onset channels [LIT-ccep-9][LIT-ccep-54], and variability predicts the seizure-onset zone with AUC 0.71–0.74 [LIT-ccep-55].
- Sleep and anaesthesia modulate responses [LIT-ccep-12][LIT-ccep-56][LIT-ccep-57][LIT-ccep-58].
- Delayed responses at 100 ms to 1 s are the oldest SPES biomarker, but no automated detector exists for them [LIT-ccep-19][LIT-ccep-60][LIT-ccep-61].

**Data and code.** Public datasets are BIDS-iEEG and CC0, led by a 74-patient ECoG set [LIT-ccep-64]. Lab code is mostly MATLAB [LIT-ccep-59][LIT-ccep-65]. The BIDS standard has no controlled fields for pulse width, polarity or pulse count [LIT-ccep-66][LIT-ccep-67]. ERPparam is listed among the tools [LIT-ccep-68], but it was not designed for stimulation data.

**Implications.** A tool for this field should provide:
- a registry of every published detection rule, under named and versioned detectors
- a component model that gives timing and shape with uncertainty
- a public benchmark with ground truth
- re-referencing and reference-sensitivity reports
- per-trial and dose-response models with state covariates
- polarity-agnostic fitting
- a reporting checklist

## 4. Stimulation artifact removal

Source file: `research/artifact-removal.md`. Keys: `[LIT-artifact-1]` to `[LIT-artifact-71]`.

### Synthesis

This axis compares methods that remove stimulation artifacts across electrical, magnetic and optogenetic paradigms. It covers their equations, their validation, their licences and their Python availability.

**Method families.**
- *Blanking and interpolation* is still the default in CCEP and TMS-EEG pipelines [LIT-artifact-8][LIT-artifact-9][LIT-artifact-11]. All four TMS-EEG pipelines compared by Bertazzoli start with it [LIT-artifact-7].
- *Template subtraction* has global, moving and amplitude-scaled variants [LIT-artifact-19][LIT-artifact-20][LIT-artifact-21]. The sensing-implant pipeline adds a matched filter and adaptive filtering [LIT-artifact-13].
- *Periodic reconstruction* (PARRM) averages phase-aligned samples of a periodic stimulus [LIT-artifact-1]. It has a MATLAB reference implementation with no licence [LIT-artifact-2], a Python port [LIT-artifact-3] and a pending patent application [LIT-artifact-4].
- *Matching-pursuit reconstruction* (MPARRM) handles single pulses [LIT-artifact-5] and is the subject of its own patent application [LIT-artifact-25].
- *Array methods*: cascaded PCA regression across channels, pulses and trials (ERAASR) [LIT-artifact-6][LIT-artifact-26], and pre-whitening with null projection (PWNP) [LIT-artifact-14].
- *Local polynomial subtraction* (SALPA) gives real-time recovery after saturation on microelectrode arrays [LIT-artifact-27][LIT-artifact-28].
- *ICA-based pipelines* (TESA, ARTIST, TMSEEG) [LIT-artifact-8][LIT-artifact-10][LIT-artifact-11], and the source-informed SSP-SIR and SOUND methods [LIT-artifact-15][LIT-artifact-16].
- *Kalman and adaptive filters*, which are the basis of hardware cancellers in bidirectional devices [LIT-artifact-32][LIT-artifact-33].
- *Paradigm-level cancellation* in cochlear-implant ECAP, by alternating polarity or forward masking [LIT-artifact-36][LIT-artifact-37].

Two further fields handle artifacts differently:
- In spinal-cord-stimulation ECAP, artifact management is mostly hardware and proprietary firmware, documented in device manuals and trial reports [LIT-artifact-39][LIT-artifact-41].
- In DBS, evoked resonant neural activity (ERNA) is read 4–20 ms after the last pulse of a burst, so the paradigm itself avoids the artifact [LIT-artifact-17].

**Optogenetic photoelectric artifacts** are reduced mainly by hardware design. Shielding, substrate doping and pulse shaping bring them below spike-detection thresholds [LIT-artifact-22][LIT-artifact-23][LIT-artifact-24].

**Validation is not comparable across methods.**
- PARRM was validated in saline with known injected sinusoids [LIT-artifact-1].
- PWNP and a TMS-EEG standardisation study injected real artifact templates into clean data with a known signal [LIT-artifact-14][LIT-artifact-12].
- Test-retest agreement across pipelines is poor for early TEP peaks: concordance −0.03 to 0.59 before 100 ms [LIT-artifact-7].

Each method uses its own metric: relative RMSE, signal-to-interference ratio, concordance or correlation. No cross-modality benchmark with ground truth exists [LIT-artifact-1][LIT-artifact-6][LIT-artifact-7][LIT-artifact-12][LIT-artifact-14]. A 2024 review evaluates stimulation-artifact removal for evoked responses [LIT-fitting-16]. Microstimulation and ECG comparisons address narrower settings [LIT-artifact-58][LIT-artifact-68][LIT-artifact-70][LIT-artifact-71].

**Licensing and Python status.**
- Copyleft or unlicensed: TMSEEG, CCEP_Toolbox and its Python port, mnl_ieegBasics, SALPA, ERAASR, the MATLAB PARRM code, and TESA, for which no licence was detected [LIT-artifact-11][LIT-artifact-49][LIT-artifact-50][LIT-artifact-51][LIT-artifact-28][LIT-artifact-26][LIT-artifact-2][LIT-artifact-8].
- Permissive: PyPARRM, the Gaussian-process spike-separation code, pyeCAP (LGPL), perceive and BEST [LIT-artifact-3][LIT-artifact-54][LIT-artifact-55][LIT-artifact-42][LIT-artifact-44][LIT-artifact-46].
- Python offers only fragments: PyPARRM for periodic artifacts, small TMS-EEG ports [LIT-artifact-64][LIT-artifact-65][LIT-artifact-66], SpikeInterface's trigger-window replacement [LIT-artifact-62] and MNE's window interpolation [LIT-artifact-63].
- A UAB laboratory's CCEP detection repository has no licence [LIT-artifact-67].

**Implications.**
- A permissively licensed library that covers these methods behind one interface with per-sample validity masks does not exist.
- Early-latency windows, where pipelines disagree most and where ECAP, ERNA and N1 live, should be treated as missing data in the downstream fit, not as clean interpolated samples.
- Artifact and response overlap in single-pulse data, which argues for a joint fit.
- Saturation, implanted-device constraints and paradigm-level cancellation need explicit support.
- Provenance of every replaced sample should be written out.
- The two patent applications [LIT-artifact-4][LIT-artifact-25] and the unlicensed reference code call for clean-room re-implementation from the published equations.

## 5. Curve fitting, model selection and uncertainty

Source file: `research/fitting-methods.md`. Keys: `[LIT-fitting-1]` to `[LIT-fitting-55]`.

### Synthesis

This axis covers the statistical machinery a parametric evoked-response model needs:
- shape families
- background terms
- choosing the number of components
- identifiability
- uncertainty
- hierarchical structure
- robust losses
- multi-channel constraints
- the Python tooling that can carry all of this

**Shapes.** The Gaussian is the default peak and is exactly the periodic component of FOOOF/specparam [LIT-fitting-13][LIT-fitting-14]. Other shapes fit specific signals better:
- Skew-normal and ex-Gaussian kernels capture the fast-rise, slow-decay asymmetry of the CCEP N1 and the TEP N100. The ex-Gaussian also links latency distributions to reaction-time models [LIT-fitting-27].
- Gamma, alpha and bi-exponential kernels give causal onsets with synaptic interpretations.
- Damped sinusoids describe DBS evoked resonant neural activity [LIT-fitting-4][LIT-fitting-9]. They have a long VEP/ERP history and are known to be sensitive to noise [LIT-fitting-24][LIT-fitting-25][LIT-fitting-26].
- Gabor atoms underlie matching pursuit [LIT-fitting-23].

Shape-free approaches exist as well:
- CRP treats each trial as a scaled canonical shape [LIT-fitting-1].
- Basis profile curves cluster stimulation sites by shared motifs [LIT-fitting-2].
- Several lines of work treat each trial as a scaled, shifted template: the random-shift Gaussian process RPAGP [LIT-fitting-3], RIDE [LIT-fitting-20][LIT-fitting-21], Woody filtering [LIT-fitting-29] and regression approaches [LIT-fitting-19].

**Background and drift.** Three slow processes coexist after a pulse:
- the artifact tail
- physiological drift
- genuine slow components

Polynomial and spline backgrounds are linear in their coefficients. Variable projection can therefore solve the background and all amplitudes in closed form for any fixed nonlinear parameters. Exponential recovery terms need bounds from the hardware, because they are nearly collinear with an early component. CRP lists drift and offset as failure modes [LIT-fitting-1]. Early CCEP components overlap the artifact tail [LIT-fitting-15][LIT-fitting-16].

**Number of components.** FOOOF/specparam adds peaks against thresholds up to a cap [LIT-fitting-13][LIT-fitting-14]. Hidden multivariate pattern models instead compare models with increasing numbers of events [LIT-fitting-10][LIT-fitting-22]. AIC and BIC over-select when residuals are autocorrelated unless the effective sample size or the noise model is corrected. The alternatives are:
- cross-validation and LOO [LIT-fitting-32]
- Bayes factors by bridge or nested sampling [LIT-fitting-33]
- Laplace approximations

**Identifiability.** Sums of kernels are sloppy models [LIT-fitting-34]. Profile likelihood separates structural from practical non-identifiability [LIT-fitting-35]. Two components closer than about one width cannot be resolved at ordinary SNR, and no ERP tool reports this.

**Uncertainty comes at three costs.**
- Jacobian (Laplace) errors are cheap, but they are too narrow under coloured noise.
- Bootstrap over trials and jackknife over participants [LIT-fitting-7][LIT-fitting-8][LIT-fitting-28] are the established resampling methods.
- Full posteriors are feasible for one waveform. Hierarchical waveform models are slow: RPAGP took about 10 hours per subject [LIT-fitting-3].
- Bayesian single-trial estimation [LIT-fitting-18], Bayesian evoked/induced estimation [LIT-fitting-43] and multichannel single-trial estimation [LIT-fitting-46] cover parts of this space.

**Hierarchy.** The current psychophysiology standard fits mixed models to extracted amplitudes [LIT-fitting-6][LIT-fitting-30][LIT-fitting-37][LIT-fitting-36]. Hierarchical models of the whole waveform exist only for nonparametric templates [LIT-fitting-3][LIT-fitting-42]. No trial-to-subject-to-population model with parametric components was found.

**Robust losses and multi-channel structure.**
- Robust losses are built into SciPy's least-squares solver [LIT-fitting-39], and robust regression improves sensitivity in neuroimaging [LIT-fitting-17]. No evoked-response fitter uses them.
- Shared-latency, channel-weighted models are established outside the stimulation literature [LIT-fitting-45][LIT-fitting-46].

**Tooling.**
- SciPy and lmfit provide the solvers and many shapes [LIT-fitting-47][LIT-fitting-48].
- JAX and numba allow batching over trials and channels [LIT-fitting-49][LIT-fitting-50], and CuPy offers GPU arrays [LIT-fitting-51].
- NumPyro, PyMC, CmdStanPy and ArviZ cover Bayesian inference [LIT-fitting-40][LIT-fitting-38][LIT-fitting-52][LIT-fitting-44].
- Versions and licences are listed in [LIT-fitting-55].
- In the target domains, TEP tools only pick peaks [LIT-fitting-11][LIT-fitting-12]. Methodological choices change TEP results [LIT-fitting-54], and half of CCEP studies do not state their detection method [LIT-fitting-53].

**Implications.**
- a declarative sum-of-components model over a slow background, solved by variable projection
- model selection on whitened residuals
- uncertainty at three cost levels
- a hierarchical parametric model
- identifiability diagnostics as an output
- joint artifact-tail fitting
- damped-oscillation components
- robust losses
- shared-latency multi-channel fits
- batched backends for speed

## 6. Open datasets for evoked responses

Source file: `research/datasets.md`. Keys: `[LIT-datasets-1]` to `[LIT-datasets-75]`.

### Synthesis

This axis inventories public data that can validate and benchmark an evoked-response tool, by modality and species. It also records metadata quality and the gaps that only simulation can fill.

**Human CCEP/SPES.** The intracranial corpus on OpenNeuro is small in number of datasets but large in volume. All of it is BIDS-iEEG and CC0, and it comes mainly from two groups.
- ds004080 is the reference set: 74 patients aged 4–51, ECoG at 2048 Hz, 269 GB [LIT-datasets-1][LIT-datasets-2]. It has a NEMAR mirror with chunked copies [LIT-datasets-3] and is catalogued in EEG-DaSh [LIT-datasets-4].
- Four Mayo Clinic sets share one protocol (single pulses at about 0.2 Hz, 200 µs, 6 mA): ds003708, ds004457, ds004696 and ds004977. This makes them a natural multi-dataset benchmark [LIT-datasets-5][LIT-datasets-7][LIT-datasets-9][LIT-datasets-11]. Their companion papers cover basis profile curves, limbic stimulation, network interactions and adjusted common averaging [LIT-datasets-6][LIT-datasets-8][LIT-datasets-10][LIT-datasets-12].
- ds006254 has 31 sEEG subjects but thin dataset-level metadata, and its events file status is unconfirmed [LIT-datasets-13]. ds007703 could not be characterised [LIT-datasets-14].
- Further stimulation sets cover negative motor responses and invasive spinal recordings [LIT-datasets-15][LIT-datasets-16].
- Outside OpenNeuro: a thalamocortical SPES set on Zenodo [LIT-datasets-18][LIT-datasets-19]; the F-TRACT atlas, which holds features rather than waveforms [LIT-datasets-20][LIT-datasets-21][LIT-datasets-22][LIT-datasets-23]; and Localize-MI, simultaneous intracerebral stimulation with high-density EEG [LIT-datasets-24][LIT-datasets-25][LIT-datasets-26].

**Scalp ERP and BCI.**
- ERP CORE provides 40 participants, seven components in six paradigms, and published pipelines and scores [LIT-datasets-29][LIT-datasets-30][LIT-datasets-31]. A Python fetcher exists [LIT-datasets-71].
- Classic multimodal and oddball sets [LIT-datasets-32][LIT-datasets-33][LIT-datasets-34] and EEGManyPipelines [LIT-datasets-35][LIT-datasets-36] add analytic-variability data.
- P300 speller datasets supply single-trial material [LIT-datasets-37][LIT-datasets-38][LIT-datasets-39].

**TMS-EEG and tES.** Only one TMS-EEG dataset was found on OpenNeuro, with minimal metadata [LIT-datasets-17]. Larger sets are deposited with data papers [LIT-datasets-40][LIT-datasets-41]. The TESA example data are also available [LIT-datasets-42], as is a tES-EEG dose dataset [LIT-datasets-72].

**Neuromodulation.**
- No public DBS-evoked (ERNA) dataset was located. The 2025 pallidal study withholds its data [LIT-datasets-44], and the other ERNA papers have no deposit [LIT-datasets-45][LIT-datasets-46].
- No public SCS ECAP dataset was located, human or animal [LIT-datasets-47][LIT-datasets-48][LIT-datasets-49].

**Animals.**
- The Allen Visual Coding Neuropixels release is the only large, fully open, NWB-formatted source of sensory-evoked LFP [LIT-datasets-50][LIT-datasets-51][LIT-datasets-52]. It is distributed through DANDI [LIT-datasets-53].
- Utah-array sets are resting or behavioural [LIT-datasets-54][LIT-datasets-55], and a rat anaesthesia set offers high-density LFP [LIT-datasets-56].
- No electrical-microstimulation LFP dataset was found.

**Artifacts.**
- The only phantom EEG dataset covers motion, muscle, eye and line noise, not electrical stimulation [LIT-datasets-57].
- Artifact-removal papers do not appear to deposit their validation recordings [LIT-datasets-58][LIT-datasets-59][LIT-datasets-60][LIT-datasets-61].

**Labels and reviews.**
- ER-detect provides a detection reference [LIT-datasets-62]. Recent reviews and a localization study catalogue methods [LIT-datasets-65][LIT-datasets-66][LIT-datasets-67]. No public set of expert labels for response existence, latency and amplitude exists across datasets.
- Further stimulation studies hold relevant recordings [LIT-datasets-73][LIT-datasets-74][LIT-datasets-75].

**Metadata quality.**
- Sampling rate and channel count sit only in per-run sidecars.
- Stimulation columns follow a convention in ds004080 and in the Mayo sets but are absent or unconfirmed elsewhere [LIT-datasets-2][LIT-datasets-5][LIT-datasets-13].
- BIDS gives stimulation fields only as recommendations [LIT-datasets-68].
- The open-data lists [LIT-datasets-27] and catalogues lag behind new deposits [LIT-datasets-4].

**Implications.**
- Simulation generators for ERNA, ECAP and artifacts
- An events-harmonisation layer and a stimulation-metadata validator
- A labelled CCEP benchmark built from the CC0 sets
- NWB loaders validated on Allen data
- TMS-EEG adapters
- F-TRACT distributions used as priors or plausibility checks
- A machine-readable dataset registry
- Lazy readers for 269 GB deposits

## 7. Other evoked-response domains (TMS-EEG, DBS, IONM, peripheral, BCI)

Source file: `research/other-domains.md`. Keys: `[LIT-domains-1]` to `[LIT-domains-86]`.

### Synthesis

This axis looks beyond CCEP and scalp ERP to the other communities that quantify evoked responses. For each one it asks what is measured, with which software, under which regulatory constraints, and what is missing.

**TMS-EEG.**
- The consensus TEP components after motor-cortex stimulation run from N15 to P180 [LIT-domains-1][LIT-domains-2].
- Quantification is peak picking in fixed windows on top of MATLAB pipelines: TESA, TMSEEG and ARTIST, with SSP-SIR and SOUND for cleaning [LIT-domains-4][LIT-domains-5][LIT-domains-6][LIT-domains-7][LIT-domains-8][LIT-domains-9].
- These pipelines give different results. A ground-truth comparison and a test-retest study show that pipeline choice changes TEP amplitudes [LIT-domains-3][LIT-domains-10], and a systematic comparison of motor-cortex studies reaches the same conclusion [LIT-domains-11].
- Separating transcranial from sensory-evoked components remains an open problem [LIT-domains-12].
- Public TMS-EEG data exist but are scattered across repositories [LIT-domains-14][LIT-domains-15][LIT-domains-16][LIT-domains-17][LIT-domains-18].

**Clinical evoked potentials and IONM.**
- SSEP, VEP, BAEP and MEP measures are defined by guidelines [LIT-domains-19][LIT-domains-20][LIT-domains-21][LIT-domains-22]. They are computed inside vendor systems cleared as class II devices [LIT-domains-23][LIT-domains-24].
- The ISIN recommendations reject fixed alarm rules (50% amplitude loss, 10% latency increase). They ask instead for changes that clearly exceed trial-to-trial variability [LIT-domains-25]. Teaching summaries still use the fixed percentages [LIT-domains-27], and an updated SEP position statement is available [LIT-domains-28].
- Automated SSEP assessment is sold only as a closed cleared device [LIT-domains-26].
- Market reports place IONM at about US$3.65–3.75 billion in 2025 [LIT-domains-34][LIT-domains-35][LIT-domains-36]. No public IONM recordings were found.

**Spinal cord stimulation ECAP.**
- The dorsal-column ECAP is the feedback signal of closed-loop systems [LIT-domains-37][LIT-domains-38][LIT-domains-39][LIT-domains-42][LIT-domains-43]. The Evoke system adjusts current for every stimulus.
- Clinical follow-up and device reviews exist [LIT-domains-40][LIT-domains-41][LIT-domains-46][LIT-domains-47][LIT-domains-48][LIT-domains-49][LIT-domains-50]. Preclinical closed-loop work exists in rats [LIT-domains-44][LIT-domains-45].
- Detection and artifact handling are proprietary. No open SCS-ECAP analysis package or dataset was found.

**DBS ERNA.**
- ERNA is a decaying 200–450 Hz oscillation evoked by subthalamic stimulation [LIT-domains-52][LIT-domains-53].
- Features are defined ad hoc: two quantification routes correlate at ρ = 0.93–0.98, other studies use different feature sets, and links to clinical outcome are inconsistent [LIT-domains-53][LIT-domains-54].
- Pallidal [LIT-domains-55][LIT-domains-56][LIT-domains-57] and large-animal [LIT-domains-58] work extends the field, and automated programming based on ERNA has been reported [LIT-domains-59]. Sensing-enabled implants make chronic recording possible [LIT-domains-60].

**Cochlear-implant ECAP.**
- Clinical ECAP thresholds are measured with forward-masking or alternating-polarity paradigms [LIT-domains-61].
- Automation such as AutoNRT is embedded in vendor software and protected by patents [LIT-domains-62][LIT-domains-64]. AI methods for cochlear implants have been reviewed [LIT-domains-63].

**BCI.**
- MOABB, pyRiemann, BCI2000 and OpenViBE classify single trials but do not parameterize them [LIT-domains-66][LIT-domains-67][LIT-domains-68][LIT-domains-69].
- Large open P300 and ErrP datasets exist [LIT-domains-71][LIT-domains-72][LIT-domains-73][LIT-domains-74].

**Animals.**
- DANDI hosts NWB data at scale [LIT-domains-76]. The Allen survey and its data-sharing paper are the largest open sensory-evoked resource [LIT-domains-77][LIT-domains-79].
- Analysis of these data stops at rate metrics, for example in optotagging [LIT-domains-78].

**Closed loop.** State-dependent TMS [LIT-domains-80] and DBS-evoked cortical potentials [LIT-domains-81] show that evoked parameters are used online. Updating at this rate needs a fast, deterministic estimator.

**Regulation.**
- Clinical-decision software falls under FDA guidance [LIT-domains-82] and EU MDCG guidance [LIT-domains-83], with IEC 62304 for the software life cycle [LIT-domains-84].
- Evoked-potential analysis devices are cleared through the 510(k) route [LIT-domains-85].
- A research package is not a device. It can, however, be designed to be a credible component for vendors who carry that burden.

**Reference point.** FOOOF/specparam has 2,533 citations (OpenAlex, 2026-10-07) [LIT-domains-86]. It shows how far a well-defined parameterization can travel across communities.

**Implications.**
- a variability-aware IONM change statistic with calibrated probabilities
- a TEP component model that consumes existing cleaning outputs
- damped-oscillation ERNA models
- an open ECAP reference implementation
- single-trial P300 parameters
- NWB-native animal workflows
- simulation ground truth for modalities without public data
- a minimal real-time estimator
- deterministic, validated algorithms ready for regulated use

## 8. Time-frequency analysis of evoked responses

Source file: `research/time-frequency.md`. Keys: `[LIT-tf-1]` to `[LIT-tf-68]`.

### Synthesis

This axis covers time-frequency (TF) parameterization of stimulation-evoked responses. It focuses on cortico-cortical spectral responses (CCSR) and on the methodological choices that make TF results hard to compare.

**Evoked, induced and total power differ.** Induced activity is defined against the phase-locked evoked response [LIT-tf-21][LIT-tf-22][LIT-tf-23]. Computing a TF map of total power mixes:
- the spectral footprint of the evoked waveform
- genuine induced high gamma
- post-excitatory suppression

Subtracting the average before the transform is also biased when latency jitters [LIT-tf-22]. CRP suggests working on single-trial residuals but leaves the frequency domain to future work [LIT-tf-7].

**CCSR and its predecessors.**
- The 2024 CCSR pipeline uses a generalized Morse wavelet [LIT-tf-24], baseline normalization and pixel-wise FDR, and it counts power increases inside hand-defined zones [LIT-tf-2].
- Brinyark's 2026 thesis and an article titled on CCSR parameter optimization are related work [LIT-tf-3][LIT-tf-4]. The article could not be confirmed (its record remains unverified), so its eleven-zone scheme is not cited as fact.
- Earlier work defined high-frequency SPES responses and their links to the seizure-onset zone [LIT-tf-1][LIT-tf-5][LIT-tf-28][LIT-tf-29]. Delayed high-frequency suppression identifies the seizure-onset zone [LIT-tf-30]. Thalamic and cortical stimulation produce different post-excitatory suppression [LIT-tf-6].
- Graph measures and network topology have been derived from these maps [LIT-tf-11][LIT-tf-25][LIT-tf-26][LIT-tf-27].
- Two items that came up in searches remain unidentified. They are kept only to preserve the numbering and are not citable [LIT-tf-16][LIT-tf-19].

**Estimators and baselines.**
- Wavelet and multitaper estimators trade time against frequency resolution on 10–500 ms responses, and smearing depends on the sampling rate and the Nyquist limit.
- Single-trial division by the baseline skews the distribution [LIT-tf-12][LIT-tf-36]. Baseline normalization modes differ in bias [LIT-tf-67][LIT-tf-68], and the MNE and FieldTrip implementations expose different choices [LIT-tf-34][LIT-tf-35].
- At 1 Hz stimulation the baseline window can overlap the previous pulse's delayed response.

**Statistics.**
- CCSR work uses pixel-wise FDR. Only 16.7% of CCEP studies use any statistical test [LIT-tf-9].
- Cluster permutation [LIT-tf-10], TFCE [LIT-tf-38][LIT-tf-39] and the caution that clusters do not establish latency or location [LIT-tf-37][LIT-tf-40] are established but unused in this field.

**Decomposition.**
- PCA, PARAFAC, tensor and NMF families [LIT-tf-41][LIT-tf-42][LIT-tf-43][LIT-tf-44][LIT-tf-45][LIT-tf-46] could replace fixed zones with data-driven ones.
- Basis profile curves already cluster time-domain responses [LIT-tf-47].

**Spectral parameterization of event-related data.**
- FOOOF/specparam [LIT-tf-48][LIT-tf-13] and its time-resolved variant SPRiNT use windows of about 1 s with averaging [LIT-tf-8], which is too long for most stimulation responses.
- Model selection for peaks [LIT-tf-49], aperiodic contributions to ERSP effects [LIT-tf-50], eBOSC [LIT-tf-51], PAPTO [LIT-tf-52] and IRASA [LIT-tf-53] are the main alternatives. Recommendations exist on the pitfalls [LIT-tf-54].

**ECoG versus sEEG.**
- Montage [LIT-tf-55][LIT-tf-56][LIT-tf-60][LIT-tf-61][LIT-tf-62], white-matter contacts [LIT-tf-57][LIT-tf-58] and contact depth [LIT-tf-63] change both evoked and spectral responses.
- Stimulation parameters change them too [LIT-tf-18][LIT-tf-33].
- Guidelines call for complete reporting [LIT-tf-59], and a draft ACNS guideline addresses stimulation practice [LIT-tf-17].
- Network and connectivity studies show the same sensitivity [LIT-tf-14][LIT-tf-15][LIT-tf-31][LIT-tf-32].

**Animal evoked spectral responses** [LIT-tf-64][LIT-tf-65][LIT-tf-66] are not yet integrated with the human SPES literature.

**Note on status.** Most entries still marked UNVERIFIED in this axis are bibliographic records of foundational, widely used methods; their DOIs and page ranges have not been confirmed against a registry and should be checked before citation.

**Implications.**
- Compute TF maps of the fitted evoked model, the residual and total power, so that the evoked footprint is explicit.
- Offer data-driven zones together with a compatibility layer for published zones.
- Check baselines against the stimulation design.
- Provide cluster and TFCE inference.
- Report signed metrics for suppression.
- Benchmark short-window spectral fits.
- Treat montage and tissue as covariates.
- Provide a reference CCSR implementation and per-trial TF loadings.

## 9. Trial-by-trial variability, brain state and animal evoked responses

Source file: `research/variability-brainstate.md`. Keys: `[LIT-variability-1]` to `[LIT-variability-82]`.

### Synthesis

This axis covers how evoked responses vary from trial to trial, how brain state and stimulation parameters drive that variation, and how far the same shape models carry over to animal recordings.

**A response is not a fixed fingerprint.**
- The F-TRACT second-order probability analysis paired 7,079 stimulation trains from 445 patients. At a detection threshold of Z = 3, agreement between two identical trains was near zero; it exceeded a correlation of 0.5 only at Z = 5. Beyond 90 mm the correlation at Z = 3 was 0.04 [LIT-variability-7].
- Thresholds remain the dominant method: 68.1% of the 72 studies with an explicit method used them [LIT-variability-9]. A second review reaches the same conclusion from the epileptogenic-zone side [LIT-variability-10].
- Cornblath et al. computed single-trial N1 and N2 amplitudes over 30 trials at 1 Hz. Amplitudes drifted monotonically, and seizure-onset electrodes showed a lower mean but a higher trial-to-trial SD. The authors argue that averaging can mask exactly the information of interest [LIT-variability-1][LIT-variability-57].

**Existing single-trial measures.**
- Block averages of ten pulses, scored as peak-to-peak amplitude in a 10–60 ms window, are the de facto plasticity metric [LIT-variability-4].
- CRP projects each trial onto one canonical shape and yields a weight, a residual and a significance test, but it has no latency term [LIT-variability-2].
- Its application summarises reliability as the median across-trial R², reported without intervals. The accompanying public BIDS dataset is suitable for benchmarking [LIT-variability-3][LIT-variability-72].
- Population-level neural-mass fits align the N1 to within 1.9 ms [LIT-variability-8].

No study opened for this review reports a trial-level CCEP N1 latency SD. This is a documented gap, not a known small number. Scalp ERP research has a long line of latency-jitter estimators [LIT-variability-27][LIT-variability-28][LIT-variability-29][LIT-variability-30][LIT-variability-31][LIT-variability-32][LIT-variability-75][LIT-variability-76], mostly in MATLAB.

**State dependence.**
- *Sleep.* Sleep changes CCEP amplitude and morphology [LIT-variability-11][LIT-variability-12][LIT-variability-13]. The bistability account places the state-sensitive part of the response in its late components [LIT-variability-14][LIT-variability-15].
- *Pre-stimulus mu phase.* In real-time phase-triggered TMS, negative-peak trials produced MEPs about 1.33 times larger, with a phase-estimation error of about 45° [LIT-variability-16]. Phase estimation itself is uncertain [LIT-variability-18][LIT-variability-20], and in some paradigms power predicts better than phase [LIT-variability-21][LIT-variability-22][LIT-variability-62]. See also [LIT-variability-17][LIT-variability-19][LIT-variability-63].
- *Pre-stimulus power and perception.* Iemi et al. separate additive baseline effects from multiplicative gain [LIT-variability-23][LIT-variability-24][LIT-variability-25]. This distinction maps directly onto a model of components over a slow background.
- *Anaesthesia.* In rodents, anaesthesia scales evoked amplitude by large factors: up to 302% enlargement of the mouse VEP N1 [LIT-variability-49], and dose-dependent isoflurane suppression together with changes in within-train adaptation in the rat [LIT-variability-48][LIT-variability-51]. Human intra-operative data show propofol effects [LIT-variability-64].
- *Arousal and attention.* Arousal and attention modulate both amplitude and reliability [LIT-variability-26][LIT-variability-50][LIT-variability-61].

**Stimulation parameters.**
- Local responses rise steeply with current and peak at 5–8 mA, while distant responses plateau [LIT-variability-5].
- Below 5.5 mA responses are unreliable [LIT-variability-6].
- Pulse width acts nonlinearly [LIT-variability-36][LIT-variability-65], and polarity, pulse shape and referencing all matter [LIT-variability-38][LIT-variability-39][LIT-variability-43].
- Because amplitude drifts even at 1 Hz, trials within a train are not exchangeable [LIT-variability-1]. Habituation and recovery over a train are documented in animals [LIT-variability-47][LIT-variability-48].

**Animals and tools.**
- In NHP microstimulation, an early excitatory response is followed by long inhibition [LIT-variability-46][LIT-variability-47]. Mouse electrically evoked EEG links late components to the thalamus [LIT-variability-45].
- The time scales differ between species, so a shared model needs species-specific priors rather than fixed windows.
- Elephant, SpikeInterface and pynapple align, average or clean data, but none of them fits, tests or parameterizes evoked responses [LIT-variability-54][LIT-variability-55][LIT-variability-56].
- The parallel case of spectral parameterization [LIT-variability-71] shows that a parameterization tool can become a shared vocabulary.

**Implications.** Trial-level fitting should report dispersion as well as means. It should carry state covariates (sleep stage, pre-stimulus phase and power) and propagate their uncertainty. It should model drift within a train and dose-response explicitly, and it should allow latency jitter as a parameter. None of these is available in an open, permissively licensed Python tool today.

## 10. Package naming, licensing and intellectual property

Source file: `research/naming-licensing.md`. Keys: `[LIT-naming-1]` to `[LIT-naming-60]`.

### Synthesis

This axis checks whether candidate package names are available and which licence and intellectual-property options fit a tool derived from Apache-2.0 code at a university that claims ownership of software. Registry checks date from 2026-10-06.

**Name availability.**
- All of the following were free on PyPI: evoparam, evoshape, evofit, evoresponse, evopeaks, evoquant, evocurve, evoprofile and evomodel [LIT-naming-1].
- evokit is taken by an active evolutionary-computing package [LIT-naming-2]. Under PEP 503 normalisation and PEP 541, its variant spellings cannot be used either [LIT-naming-54].
- No conda-forge feedstock exists for any candidate [LIT-naming-3][LIT-naming-4].
- evoparam has no record in the .com or .org registries [LIT-naming-7][LIT-naming-8]. The .io registry could not be queried [LIT-naming-9].
- The exact GitHub handle "evoparam" belongs to an existing user [LIT-naming-55][LIT-naming-6]. The project therefore needs a suffixed organisation, such as evoparam-tools, in the style of fooof-tools and mne-tools.

**Conflicts.**
- evofit collides with an established forensic-psychology product, fitness marks and 85 repositories [LIT-naming-10][LIT-naming-5].
- evoshape is the name of a skull implant sold into the same neurosurgical market [LIT-naming-11].
- The "evo-" prefix belongs to evolutionary computation in general [LIT-naming-12], which costs some searchability. Pairing the name with the phrase "evoked response parameterization" recovers it, as "spectral parameterization" does for specparam.
- A scored comparison puts evoparam first at 22 of 25, with evoresponse as the fallback.
- No USPTO search was possible, so a trademark search remains outstanding before any filing [LIT-naming-59][LIT-naming-60].

**Licences.**
- *Inherited code.* specparam/FOOOF, the parent of ERPparam, is Apache-2.0 on PyPI and GitHub [LIT-naming-38]. Section 4 requires derived files to keep their notices and to carry change statements. Section 3 grants a patent licence, and Section 6 withholds trademark rights [LIT-naming-13].
- *Apache-2.0 and the GPL.* Apache-2.0 code may enter GPLv3 projects but not GPLv2-only ones [LIT-naming-14][LIT-naming-15].
- *MPL-2.0 and AGPL-3.0.* MPL-2.0 offers file-level copyleft [LIT-naming-16]. AGPL-3.0 extends copyleft to network use [LIT-naming-17], but it would bar the tool from the BSD and Apache ecosystem.
- *Ecosystem.* MNE-Python and MNE-BIDS are BSD-3-Clause [LIT-naming-39][LIT-naming-40][LIT-naming-56]. The large neighbours vary:
  - permissive: NeuroKit2, SpikeInterface, PyNWB and PyMC [LIT-naming-46][LIT-naming-47][LIT-naming-48][LIT-naming-49]
  - GPL: FieldTrip, Brainstorm, ERPLAB and SPM [LIT-naming-41][LIT-naming-43][LIT-naming-45][LIT-naming-50]
  - licence not detected: EEGLAB and TESA [LIT-naming-42][LIT-naming-44]
- *The CCEP niche.* It is almost entirely GPL-3.0 or unlicensed. This includes a Python detector [LIT-naming-51], several MATLAB toolboxes and a repository from the project's own institution that carries no licence [LIT-naming-52]. The GPL FAQ and the Apache position mean that such code can only be called at arm's length [LIT-naming-57].
- *Licence and adoption.* Large-scale licence studies find that permissive licences dominate, with Apache-2.0 rising [LIT-naming-18][LIT-naming-19]. Older econometric studies relate licence choice to development activity [LIT-naming-20][LIT-naming-21][LIT-naming-22]. A practical guide frames the choice for scientists [LIT-naming-23]. None of these sources links licence choice to citation counts. JOSS requires only an OSI-approved licence [LIT-naming-24].

**Intellectual property.**
- *Ownership and disclosure.* The university's technology-transfer office states that software made with institutional resources belongs to the university, and that disclosure must precede any public release [LIT-naming-25][LIT-naming-26][LIT-naming-27][LIT-naming-28][LIT-naming-29].
- *Grace periods.* US law gives a one-year grace period for the inventor's own disclosure, while the European Patent Convention requires absolute novelty [LIT-naming-30][LIT-naming-31]. Federal funding adds reporting duties [LIT-naming-32].
- *PARRM.* A US patent application on period-based artifact removal is pending [LIT-naming-33]. The method paper [LIT-naming-34] and its single-pulse variant MPARRM [LIT-naming-35] have reference code in MATLAB with no licence [LIT-naming-36][LIT-naming-37]. A patent search for the matching-pursuit variant was not run [LIT-naming-58].
- *The Apache patent grant.* Because Apache-2.0 grants patent rights for each contribution, releasing an implementation of a patentable method under it licenses that use to everyone. The technology-transfer conversation comes first.

**Citation vehicle.** The name is what citations attach to. The parent tool's paper has 2,533 citations (OpenAlex, 2026-10-07; 600 in 2025) [LIT-naming-53]. A single stable name with one DOI from the first release is the cheapest step toward the same outcome.

**Implications.**
- Register evoparam on PyPI, as the evoparam-tools organisation and under both domains.
- Keep inherited files under Apache-2.0 with a NOTICE file.
- Choose the licence of new modules with upstreaming to MNE in mind.
- Re-implement artifact methods clean-room and isolate GPL tools as optional extras.
- Disclose to the technology-transfer office before the first public commit.

## 11. ERP measurement methodology and single-trial estimation

Source file: `research/erp-measurement-methods.md`. Keys: `[LIT-erpmeas-1]` to `[LIT-erpmeas-70]`.

### Synthesis

This axis covers the classical ways of scoring averaged ERPs, the single-trial estimators that correct for latency jitter, regression-based ERPs, and the reliability and data-quality metrics that reviewers now expect.

**Scoring averaged waveforms.** The modern canon prefers mean amplitude over an a priori window. Mean amplitude is linear and unbiased by trial count. Peak amplitude, by contrast, is inflated by noise and confounds conditions that have different trial numbers [LIT-erpmeas-1][LIT-erpmeas-46].

For latency:
- *Broad components.* Fractional area latency is recommended, and a worked exercise shows larger effect sizes than with peak latency because measurement error is lower [LIT-erpmeas-46][LIT-erpmeas-45].
- *Onsets.* A relative criterion is used: the first time, searching back from the peak, at which the signal drops below a fraction of the peak amplitude. Thirty percent is preferred to ten percent [LIT-erpmeas-2][LIT-erpmeas-3].
- *Robust variants.* Liesefeld's toolbox adds adaptive area baselines and counter-peak anchoring [LIT-erpmeas-3][LIT-erpmeas-56].
- *Group latencies.* These are best estimated by jackknifing, which corrects the test statistics [LIT-erpmeas-4][LIT-erpmeas-5][LIT-erpmeas-2]. Smulders' retrieval formula returns per-subject values that can enter any standard test [LIT-erpmeas-6].
- *Latency contrasts.* Dynamic time warping gives a latency contrast without peak picking [LIT-erpmeas-47].
- *Outside MATLAB.* An R package scores latency [LIT-erpmeas-65], and MNE offers only a global peak finder [LIT-erpmeas-52].

**Latency jitter.** Trial-to-trial jitter convolves a component with its latency distribution. This lowers and broadens the averaged peak while conserving its area, so a difference in jitter can masquerade as a difference in amplitude [LIT-erpmeas-12][LIT-erpmeas-13].

The correcting estimators form one family, each a version of "shared shapes with per-trial amplitude and latency":
- *Woody's adaptive filter* [LIT-erpmeas-7] locks onto noise at low SNR.
- *Maximum-likelihood models* let both amplitude and latency vary per trial [LIT-erpmeas-8][LIT-erpmeas-9][LIT-erpmeas-50][LIT-erpmeas-51].
- *RIDE* separates stimulus-locked, response-locked and latency-variable clusters [LIT-erpmeas-10][LIT-erpmeas-11][LIT-erpmeas-12][LIT-erpmeas-13][LIT-erpmeas-14]. Its Python port has almost no adoption [LIT-erpmeas-15].
- *ASEO* adds an autoregressive model of the ongoing activity [LIT-erpmeas-16][LIT-erpmeas-59].
- *Other routes:* wavelet denoising [LIT-erpmeas-20][LIT-erpmeas-49][LIT-erpmeas-70], regression on the template and its derivatives [LIT-erpmeas-21][LIT-erpmeas-22][LIT-erpmeas-23], Kalman smoothing across trials [LIT-erpmeas-24][LIT-erpmeas-25], Bayesian regularisation [LIT-erpmeas-26] and estimation of multiple correlated components [LIT-erpmeas-62].
- *Recent models.* A Gaussian-process random-shift model formalises jitter plus amplitude variability, but it needs about 10 hours per subject and has no released code [LIT-erpmeas-27]. Hidden multivariate pattern analysis detects per-trial event onsets in Python, but it assumes a fixed half-sine shape [LIT-erpmeas-48].

Evaluations agree that SNR gating and model mismatch dominate the error [LIT-erpmeas-9][LIT-erpmeas-12].

**Decomposition and regression.**
- *Temporal PCA* separates overlapping components [LIT-erpmeas-17][LIT-erpmeas-18][LIT-erpmeas-58]. Simulations show it can misallocate variance between them [LIT-erpmeas-19].
- *LIMO EEG* fits a hierarchical GLM on single trials but does not estimate component latencies [LIT-erpmeas-28].
- *Regression ERPs* recast averaging as least squares and add overlap correction and non-linear predictors [LIT-erpmeas-29][LIT-erpmeas-30][LIT-erpmeas-55][LIT-erpmeas-53].
- *Unfold* is the reference deconvolution toolbox, with a Julia successor and a simulator [LIT-erpmeas-31][LIT-erpmeas-32][LIT-erpmeas-33][LIT-erpmeas-64]. The same tools handle combined EEG and eye-tracking data [LIT-erpmeas-69].
- *Temporal response functions* extend this to continuous stimuli [LIT-erpmeas-34][LIT-erpmeas-35][LIT-erpmeas-57].

**Reliability and data quality.**
- *Dependability.* The generalizability-theory dependability coefficient is promoted for ERP scores and implemented in the ERA Toolbox [LIT-erpmeas-36][LIT-erpmeas-37][LIT-erpmeas-38][LIT-erpmeas-67].
- *SME.* The standardized measurement error gives a per-participant, per-score precision. It is analytic for mean amplitude and bootstrapped for any non-linear score [LIT-erpmeas-41], and it has been reported across paradigms [LIT-erpmeas-42]. In 2025 it became available in commercial software and in MNE [LIT-erpmeas-54].
- *Benchmark data.* ERP CORE provides seven components in 40 participants and is the de facto benchmark [LIT-erpmeas-39][LIT-erpmeas-40][LIT-erpmeas-63].
- *Trial counts.* Fixed trial-count rules do not transfer between components or designs [LIT-erpmeas-43][LIT-erpmeas-44][LIT-erpmeas-60][LIT-erpmeas-61].
- *Analytic flexibility.* Analysis choices inflate false positives and move effect sizes [LIT-erpmeas-66][LIT-erpmeas-68].

**Implications.**
- Almost the whole measurement canon lives in MATLAB.
- A Python tool that fits shapes should also emit the classical scores, with SME, from the same object, so that its outputs can be checked against ERPLAB.
- It should treat latency jitter as an explicit parameter with uncertainty, and benchmark itself on ERP CORE.

## 12. What makes a neuroscience tool highly cited

Source file: `research/citation-success.md`. Keys: `[LIT-citation-1]` to `[LIT-citation-57]`.

### Synthesis

This axis asks what made the highly cited neuroscience tools highly cited, and which of their practices a new evoked-response tool can copy. Citation totals and per-year series come from OpenAlex. The comparison tools were retrieved on 2026-10-06. The FOOOF/specparam paper was re-retrieved on 2026-10-07 (see verification legend) [LIT-citation-33][LIT-citation-34].

**Totals and velocity.**
- *Totals.* Over two decades, the long-lived MATLAB environments accumulated the largest totals: EEGLAB 25,334, FieldTrip 11,502 and Brainstorm 3,980 (OpenAlex, 2026-10-06) [LIT-citation-5][LIT-citation-6][LIT-citation-8]. EEGLAB's annual count is still rising in its 21st year [LIT-citation-26][LIT-citation-27].
- *FOOOF/specparam.* The FOOOF/specparam paper has 2,533 citations (OpenAlex, 2026-10-07), with a per-year series for 2021–2026 of 178/283/425/506/600/472 [LIT-citation-1]. Its fifth-year rate is higher than that of every other EEG/MEG tool paper in the sample at the same age. It passed 1,000 cumulative citations in about three years, against about eight years for MNE-Python [LIT-citation-3][LIT-citation-33].
- *Preprint head start.* The 2018 preprint had already gathered 64 citations when the journal paper appeared [LIT-citation-2].
- *MNE-Python.* MNE-Python ramped slowly (7, 21 and 42 citations in its first three years) and keeps one canonical paper plus a companion paper [LIT-citation-3][LIT-citation-4].
- *MATLAB versus Python.* In 2025, EEGLAB, FieldTrip, Brainstorm and ERPLAB together received 4,532 citations, against 1,089 for the two MNE papers (OpenAlex, 2026-10-06). By that measure, roughly 80% of the citing population still works in MATLAB [LIT-citation-33].
- *ERPLAB.* ERPLAB, the closest analogue to an ERP tool, plateaued at about 300 per year. That profile is typical of a toolbox cited once per paper as an EEGLAB plugin [LIT-citation-7][LIT-citation-26].

**Stars versus citations.** Stars and citations measure different things.
- MOABB has 1,061 stars but 191 citations [LIT-citation-11][LIT-citation-32][LIT-citation-36].
- fooof has hundreds of thousands of downloads, while its citations come from papers that apply the method [LIT-citation-35].
- Library-style packages collect users. Method papers and long-lived environments convert use into citations [LIT-citation-9][LIT-citation-10][LIT-citation-12][LIT-citation-13][LIT-citation-37][LIT-citation-57].

**What the software-citation literature says.**
- Software is mentioned far more often than it is formally cited [LIT-citation-17][LIT-citation-24][LIT-citation-40][LIT-citation-41]. The FORCE11 principles and the JOSS model try to fix this [LIT-citation-18][LIT-citation-20][LIT-citation-21][LIT-citation-48].
- Even with an explicit request, only 13–26% of mentions follow the requested citation [LIT-citation-19].
- Citation targets fragment when a project changes its recommended reference [LIT-citation-23].
- Repository attributes predict citations only weakly [LIT-citation-39].
- The list of the most-cited papers of all time is dominated by methods and software [LIT-citation-25][LIT-citation-49][LIT-citation-52][LIT-citation-53][LIT-citation-54].
- Bibliometric tools face the same visibility problem [LIT-citation-22].

**Ranked practices.** The research ranks thirteen practices by strength of evidence.
1. Be a named method that every paper must cite to define its variables. Toolboxes are cited once per paper; methods are cited once per measurement.
2. Release code and a preprint early, then publish the method in a high-visibility venue [LIT-citation-1][LIT-citation-2][LIT-citation-16].
3. Keep the project alive and keep one citation target, adding per-version DOIs [LIT-citation-29][LIT-citation-23].
4. Put the citation inside the workflow. Brainstorm supplies a methods sentence [LIT-citation-30], MNE supplies BibTeX and version DOIs [LIT-citation-29][LIT-citation-31], and fMRIPrep generates a boilerplate [LIT-citation-38]. No EEG/MEG tool in the sample generates methods text automatically.
5. Integrate into the dominant environments rather than compete with them [LIT-citation-26][LIT-citation-31][LIT-citation-51].
6. Validate on simulations with known ground truth and publish the simulator [LIT-citation-9].
7. Offer a one-object API.
8. Provide tutorials on auto-downloadable public data [LIT-citation-55].
9. Publish a benchmark results paper [LIT-citation-11][LIT-citation-36].
10. Run workshops [LIT-citation-44][LIT-citation-45][LIT-citation-46]. The evidence for this one is correlational only.

Support channels [LIT-citation-28][LIT-citation-47], naming [LIT-citation-43] and licence show no measurable effect. Long-term open practice is argued for on other grounds [LIT-citation-50][LIT-citation-56].

**Implications.**
- An evoked-response tool should define named quantities with published definitions.
- It should generate its own methods paragraph, read MNE objects and export for MATLAB users.
- It should ship a simulator and a benchmark.
- It should keep one paper and one name as the citation target from the first release.

## 13. Unmet user needs in issue trackers, forums and reviews

Source file: `research/user-needs.md`. Keys: `[LIT-needs-1]` to `[LIT-needs-98]`.

### Synthesis

This axis collects unmet needs as users themselves state them in GitHub issues, forum threads and reviews. Comment counts and thread age serve as demand signals, since reactions are uniformly low.

**Classical ERP measurement in Python.**
- *The ERP Measurement Tool request.* The most persistent request in the MNE-Python tracker asked for a port of ERPLAB's measurement tool (mean amplitude, fractional peak latency and fractional area latency). It gathered 43 comments and stayed open for more than six years before closing on 2026-08-25 [LIT-needs-2].
- *What MNE ships now.* MNE now has a minimal `mne.stats.erp` namespace with peak, area, fractional latency and SME functions [LIT-needs-4]. Related requests were closed as duplicates [LIT-needs-5][LIT-needs-6].
- *`Evoked.get_peak`.* The method finds only the extremum within a window. An open issue with 14 comments discusses edge-sample warnings and a real peak detector, with no decision [LIT-needs-7].
- *Recurring confusion.* Users keep asking how to request a minimum or a maximum, and about peak semantics more generally [LIT-needs-8][LIT-needs-9][LIT-needs-10][LIT-needs-11][LIT-needs-12][LIT-needs-13]. The ERP tutorial shows only the basics [LIT-needs-3].

**The MATLAB reference.**
- *ERPLAB as the definition set.* ERPLAB's measurement tool is the de facto definition set that users want replicated [LIT-needs-14][LIT-needs-15][LIT-needs-23].
- *Open bugs.* ERPLAB itself carries open bugs in exactly the fractional measures. These include NaN returns after a zero crossing, out-of-range latencies and stray digits [LIT-needs-16][LIT-needs-17][LIT-needs-18][LIT-needs-19][LIT-needs-20][LIT-needs-21][LIT-needs-22].
- *Transparency.* Users ask how a peak is detected [LIT-needs-24], and whether the plotted standard errors are correct [LIT-needs-25].

**Model-based parameterization.**
- ERPparam is a fork of fooof with 4 stars and 18 open issues, all filed by its two developers [LIT-needs-26][LIT-needs-27]. It is in internal development rather than community use.
- Its own tracker asks for traditional ERP metrics "to aid in direct comparison between our method and traditional methods" [LIT-needs-28]. It also reports peaks dropped too liberally, slow skewed-Gaussian group fits and a need for parallel fitting [LIT-needs-27].

**Single-trial measurement.**
- *No ecosystem tool.* The MNE ecosystem has no tool for single-trial amplitude or latency. A 2017 request was closed without action [LIT-needs-29]; a trial-dependent baseline request has been open since 2018 [LIT-needs-30]; forum threads ask how to extract single trials at all [LIT-needs-31][LIT-needs-32].
- *Methods literature.* The methods exist in the literature but not as maintained Python code [LIT-needs-33][LIT-needs-34][LIT-needs-35][LIT-needs-36][LIT-needs-37][LIT-needs-38].

**Stimulation-evoked responses.**
- *Artifact handling.* MNE's only stimulation-artifact tool is window interpolation. Requests to apply it to evoked data, and a bug report against it, date from 2015 [LIT-needs-39][LIT-needs-40][LIT-needs-41][LIT-needs-42].
- *TMS-EEG.* A TMS-EEG example has been requested since 2019. The thread has 14 comments and volunteers, and nothing has been merged [LIT-needs-43]. The reference toolbox remains MATLAB [LIT-needs-44][LIT-needs-45][LIT-needs-46][LIT-needs-47].
- *CCEP and DBS ERNA.* Neither has any issue in any tracker [LIT-needs-48][LIT-needs-49][LIT-needs-50][LIT-needs-69]. Their needs appear instead in reviews and forums [LIT-needs-57][LIT-needs-58][LIT-needs-59][LIT-needs-60].
- *Stimulation metadata.* Stimulation metadata cannot round-trip through MNE-BIDS [LIT-needs-53][LIT-needs-54][LIT-needs-55], even though the iEEG specification defines stimulation fields [LIT-needs-56]. Re-referencing for sEEG is a separate open request [LIT-needs-51][LIT-needs-52].
- *Existing CCEP code.* The one Python CCEP detector is GPL and detects without parameterizing [LIT-needs-61]. The other CCEP code is MATLAB or unmaintained [LIT-needs-62][LIT-needs-63][LIT-needs-64]. Artifact-removal methods for intracranial stimulation sit in papers [LIT-needs-65][LIT-needs-66][LIT-needs-67][LIT-needs-68].

**Lessons from spectral parameterization.** The FOOOF/specparam tracker shows the lifecycle of a parameterization tool [LIT-needs-1]. Its paper has 2,533 citations (OpenAlex, 2026-10-07; 600 in 2025) [LIT-needs-70].
- *Robustness.* Fit failures and robustness dominate the tracker [LIT-needs-71][LIT-needs-72][LIT-needs-73][LIT-needs-74][LIT-needs-75][LIT-needs-76][LIT-needs-77][LIT-needs-78][LIT-needs-79][LIT-needs-80][LIT-needs-81][LIT-needs-82][LIT-needs-83].
- *Uncertainty.* Parameter uncertainty is neither offered nor requested [LIT-needs-84][LIT-needs-85]. A tool that provides it would therefore be differentiating rather than demanded.
- *Time-resolved fitting.* Users ask about time-resolved and trial-level fitting [LIT-needs-86][LIT-needs-87][LIT-needs-88].
- *Tidy output.* They want tidy output that includes peak parameters [LIT-needs-89][LIT-needs-90].
- *Benchmark.* Eight years after release, a benchmark harness is still an open maintainer issue [LIT-needs-91]. No issue asks for MNE integration [LIT-needs-92].

**Reproducibility.** Multiverse analyses show that processing and scoring choices move ERP effects [LIT-needs-93][LIT-needs-94]. Data quality varies across paradigms and participants [LIT-needs-95]. SME is the proposed per-score precision index [LIT-needs-96][LIT-needs-97][LIT-needs-98].

**Implications.**
- Users want the ERPLAB definitions in Python with correct edge cases.
- They want a real peak detector, single-trial estimates and stimulation metadata that survive BIDS.
- From the spectral precedent: invest early in failure handling, tidy outputs and a benchmark.

## 14. Standards and ecosystem integration

Source file: `research/standards-ecosystem.md`. Keys: `[LIT-standards-1]` to `[LIT-standards-100]`.

### Synthesis

This axis maps the standards and ecosystem conventions a new evoked-response package has to plug into: data formats, the APIs of neighbouring packages, citation infrastructure, publication venues, packaging practice and the options for accelerated fitting.

**Stimulation metadata in BIDS.**
- *Raw stimulation events.* The iEEG specification describes electrical stimulation through two optional sidecar fields and three example `events.tsv` columns [LIT-standards-1][LIT-standards-3][LIT-standards-93].
- *Why the columns are not enough.* The `electrical_stimulation_*` columns are not in the schema's column registry [LIT-standards-2]. As a result, units, pulse width, polarity and stimulator identity have no standardised encoding [LIT-standards-4].
- *Annotation tools.* Example datasets and HED tooling show how channel-specific annotations can be carried [LIT-standards-5][LIT-standards-6].
- *Reference dataset.* The reference CCEP dataset, ds004080, uses its own naming conventions for sessions, runs and stimulation pairs [LIT-standards-7][LIT-standards-8][LIT-standards-9][LIT-standards-95].
- *Existing tool.* The one BIDS-native CCEP tool is GPL-licensed [LIT-standards-10][LIT-standards-11].
- *Derivatives.* Derivative datasets must declare `GeneratedBy` and `SourceDatasets` [LIT-standards-12][LIT-standards-13][LIT-standards-94].
- *Proposals.* The electrophysiology derivatives proposal (BEP021) is still listed as proposed. Its drafted section covers only minimally processed data, and its pull request was closed [LIT-standards-14][LIT-standards-15][LIT-standards-16]. BIDS Stats Models is fMRI-centric [LIT-standards-17][LIT-standards-18][LIT-standards-19].

There is therefore no standard place to store fitted evoked-response parameters.

**NWB and Neo.**
- *NWB core.* NWB stores stimuli, acquisitions and trial tables, and processed results go in processing modules [LIT-standards-20][LIT-standards-21][LIT-standards-22][LIT-standards-23].
- *Events extension.* An events extension at version 0.4.0 was approved for the core standard [LIT-standards-24][LIT-standards-25][LIT-standards-26].
- *Stimulation extension.* The electrical-stimulation extension is dormant and not on PyPI [LIT-standards-27][LIT-standards-96].
- *Conversion and validation.* Conversion, inspection and archive tooling is mature [LIT-standards-28].
- *Neo and Elephant.* Neo is deliberately limited to data representation [LIT-standards-29][LIT-standards-30]. Elephant analyses on top of it but fits no evoked responses [LIT-standards-31].

**MNE integration patterns.**
- *Core library.* MNE-Python has a stable object model (Raw, Epochs, Evoked, Info, Annotations) and a citation file with version DOIs [LIT-standards-32][LIT-standards-33][LIT-standards-34][LIT-standards-92][LIT-standards-67].
- *Third-party packages.* These share one pattern: BSD licence, a citation file, PyPI plus conda-forge releases and Sphinx documentation under the MNE domain.
  - Autoreject consumes Epochs through a scikit-learn-style `fit`/`transform` [LIT-standards-35][LIT-standards-36].
  - mne-icalabel keeps heavy dependencies as extras [LIT-standards-37][LIT-standards-38].
  - mne-connectivity defines its own result containers [LIT-standards-39][LIT-standards-40].
  - MNE-BIDS and mne-lsl bridge storage and streaming [LIT-standards-41][LIT-standards-42][LIT-standards-43][LIT-standards-44].
- *The pipeline.* mne-bids-pipeline is configuration-driven with typed defaults and a fixed seed, but it offers no plugin API [LIT-standards-45][LIT-standards-46].
- *Real time.* Lab Streaming Layer and XDF are the real-time substrate [LIT-standards-47][LIT-standards-48][LIT-standards-49][LIT-standards-99].
- *The estimator contract.* The scikit-learn guide fixes the contract an estimator must honour: no logic in `__init__`, `fit` returns self, and learned attributes end in an underscore [LIT-standards-50][LIT-standards-51].

**Citation and publication.**
- *Citation infrastructure.* CITATION.cff with `preferred-citation`, plus Zenodo concept and version DOIs issued on every GitHub release, is now standard practice [LIT-standards-52][LIT-standards-53][LIT-standards-54][LIT-standards-55][LIT-standards-56][LIT-standards-57].
- *JOSS.* JOSS requires an OSI licence, a public tracker and substantial scholarly effort, including a record of public development, and limits the paper to 750–1,750 words [LIT-standards-58][LIT-standards-59][LIT-standards-60][LIT-standards-61].
- *Nature Methods.* It offers Brief Communications and Articles [LIT-standards-62]. The spectral precedent shows a method paper in a high-visibility journal combined with a stable software identity [LIT-standards-63][LIT-standards-64].
- *Other venues.* Further options include dedicated software article types [LIT-standards-65][LIT-standards-66][LIT-standards-97][LIT-standards-98].

**Engineering practice.**
- *Reproducibility.* Seeding through NumPy generators [LIT-standards-68].
- *Packaging.* A `pyproject.toml` with VCS versioning and extras, followed by a conda-forge recipe [LIT-standards-69][LIT-standards-70][LIT-standards-71][LIT-standards-72].
- *Documentation and CI.* Sphinx-Gallery documentation and matrix CI [LIT-standards-73][LIT-standards-74][LIT-standards-75][LIT-standards-76][LIT-standards-77][LIT-standards-78][LIT-standards-79][LIT-standards-100].
- *Acceleration options:*
  - Numba is stable on CPU, but its CUDA target is in maintenance mode [LIT-standards-80][LIT-standards-81].
  - JAX gives exact Jacobians and batching [LIT-standards-82]. Its solver library JAXopt is unmaintained, which leaves Optimistix, at an early version, as the active option [LIT-standards-83][LIT-standards-84][LIT-standards-85][LIT-standards-86][LIT-standards-87].
  - jaxfit is dormant [LIT-standards-88].
  - Gpufit needs compiled C++ models [LIT-standards-89].
  - lmfit, CuPy and PyTorch are further options [LIT-standards-90][LIT-standards-91].

**Implications.**
- Accept and return MNE objects, and follow the scikit-learn contract.
- Write BIDS derivatives with full provenance, and propose a parameter derivative where the standard has none.
- Read NWB.
- Release with CITATION.cff and Zenodo DOIs from the first public version.
- Keep any accelerator an optional extra behind a CPU reference path.
